by presenting the two novel intermediary results, Lemmas
C.3
and
C.6
, that were discussed at the beginning of Appendix
C
. In the first novel intermediary result, denoted below by Lemma
C.3
, we prove that the limit of the optimal objective value of the robust optimization problem (
RO
) exists almost surely as
\epsilon\to\infty
and
N\to\infty
. The proof of the following lemma is based on McDiarmid’s inequality.

Lemma C.3

Let Assumption
2.4
hold. Then for all
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\mu}\widehat{J}_{N,\epsilon}(\mu)=\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\mu}\widehat{J}_{N,\epsilon}(\mu)\quad\textnormal{almost surely}.

Proof C.4

Proof.
Consider any fixed
\epsilon>0
, and, for notational convenience, define the following function:

h_{\epsilon}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{N})\triangleq\sup_{\mu}\widehat{J}_{N,\epsilon}(\mu).

We will utilize the following intermediary claim:

Claim 3

The function
h_{\epsilon}:\mathcal{X}^{T}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{X}^{T}\to\mathbb{R}
has the following ‘bounded differences’ property: for all
\breve{x}^{1},\ldots,\breve{x}^{N}\in\mathcal{X}^{T}
and
\bar{x}^{1},\ldots,\bar{x}^{N}\in\mathcal{X}^{T}
that differ only on the
j
th coordinate (
\breve{x}^{i}=\bar{x}^{i}
for
i\neq j
),

\displaystyle\left|h_{\epsilon}(\bar{x}^{1},\ldots,\bar{x}^{N})-h_{\epsilon}(\breve{x}^{1},\ldots,\breve{x}^{N})\right|\leq\frac{U}{N}.

Proof C.5

Proof of Claim
3
. For any arbitrary
\eta>0
, let the exercise policies
\breve{\mu}^{\eta}
be chosen to satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\inf_{y\in\mathcal{X}^{T}:\;\|y-\breve{x}^{i}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon}g(\tau_{\breve{\mu}^{\eta}}(y),\breve{x}^{i})\geq h_{\epsilon}(\breve{x}^{1},\ldots,\breve{x}^{N})-\eta.

(25)

We observe that

\displaystyle h_{\epsilon}(\breve{x}^{1},\ldots,\breve{x}^{N})-h_{\epsilon}(\bar{x}^{1},\ldots,\bar{x}^{N})

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\inf_{y\in\mathcal{X}^{T}:\;\|y-\breve{x}^{i}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon}g(\tau_{\breve{\mu}^{\eta}}(y),\breve{x}^{i})+\eta\right)-\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\inf_{y\in\mathcal{X}^{T}:\;\|y-\bar{x}^{i}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon}g(\tau_{\breve{\mu}^{\eta}}(y),\bar{x}^{i})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\inf_{y\in\mathcal{X}^{T}:\;\|y-\breve{x}^{j}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon}g(\tau_{\breve{\mu}^{\eta}}(y),\breve{x}^{j})-\frac{1}{N}\inf_{y\in\mathcal{X}^{T}:\;\|y-\bar{x}^{j}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon}g(\tau_{\breve{\mu}^{\eta}}(y),\bar{x}^{j})+\eta

\displaystyle\leq\frac{U}{N}+\eta.

Indeed, the first inequality holds because of line (
25
) and because
\breve{\mu}^{\eta}
is a feasible but possibly suboptimal solution to the optimization problem
\sup_{\mu}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\inf_{y\in\mathcal{X}^{T}:\;\|y-\bar{x}^{i}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon}g(\tau_{\mu}(y),\bar{x}^{i})\equiv h_{\epsilon}(\bar{x}^{1},\ldots,\bar{x}^{N})
, and the second inequality follows from Assumption
2.4
. Because
\eta>0
was chosen arbitrarily, we have shown that

\displaystyle h_{\epsilon}(\breve{x}^{1},\ldots,\breve{x}^{N})-h_{\epsilon}(\bar{x}^{1},\ldots,\bar{x}^{N})\leq\frac{U}{N}.

It follows from symmetry that

\displaystyle h_{\epsilon}(\bar{x}^{1},\ldots,\bar{x}^{N})-h_{\epsilon}(\breve{x}^{1},\ldots,\breve{x}^{N})\leq\frac{U}{N},

which concludes our proof of Claim
3
.
\halmos

Because the above Claim
3
holds, it follows from McDiarmid’s inequality that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|h_{\epsilon}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{N})-\mathbb{E}\left[h_{\epsilon}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{N})\right]\right|>\eta\right)\leq 2\textnormal{exp}\left(-\frac{2\eta^{2}N}{U^{2}}\right)\quad\forall\eta>0.

It follows from the above line that

\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\left|h_{\epsilon}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{N})-\mathbb{E}\left[h_{\epsilon}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{N})\right]\right|>\eta\right)<\infty\quad\forall\eta>0,

and so the Borel-Cantelli lemma implies that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left|h_{\epsilon}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{N})-\mathbb{E}\left[h_{\epsilon}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{N})\right]\right|=0\quad\text{almost surely}.

(26)

We observe from identical reasoning as in the proof of
Shapiro et al. 2014
that
\mathbb{E}\left[h_{\epsilon}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{N})\right]
is monotonically decreasing with respect to
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Since the random variables
h_{\epsilon}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{N})
are also contained in the interval
[0,U]
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we conclude that
\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[h_{\epsilon}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{N})]
exists, and thus it follows from line (
26
) that
\lim_{N\to\infty}h_{\epsilon}(x^{1},\ldots,x^{N})
exists almost surely. This concludes the proof of Lemma
C.3
.
\halmos

In the second novel intermediary result, denoted below by Lemma
C.6
, we show that there always exists an arbitrarily near-optimal Markovian stopping rule
\mu
for the stochastic optimal stopping problem (
OPT
) that almost surely satisfies
\tau_{\mu}(y)=\tau_{\mu}(x)
for all sufficiently close realizations of
y\equiv(y_{1},\ldots,y_{T})
to the stochastic process
x\equiv(x_{1},\ldots,x_{T})
. The proof of the following lemma is based on elementary techniques from topology and measure theory.

Lemma C.6

Under Assumptions
2.4
and
2.4
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\mu}\mathbb{E}\left[g(\tau_{\mu}(x),x)\right]=\sup_{\mu}\mathbb{E}\left[\liminf_{y\to x}g(\tau_{\mu}(y),x)\right].

Proof C.7

Proof.
Our proof of Lemma
C.6
is organized into the following steps. In our first step, we will show that any feasible exercise policy to the stochastic optimal stopping problem (
OPT
) can be approximated to arbitrary accuracy by an exercise policy with exercise regions that are open sets. In our second step, we will show that these exercise policies from the previous step can be approximated to arbitrary accuracy by an exercise policy with exercise regions that are the union of finitely many open balls. Finally, we will then invoke Assumption
2.4
to conclude that the probability of a random state lying a strictly positive distance from the boundary of finitely many open balls is equal to one, which implies that the corresponding Markovian stopping rule almost surely satisfies
\tau_{\mu}(y)=\tau_{\mu}(x)
for all sufficiently close realizations of
y\equiv(y_{1},\ldots,y_{T})
to the stochastic process
x\equiv(x_{1},\ldots,x_{T})
. From this, the desired lemma will follow readily.

In view of the above organization, we now present our proof. Choose any arbitrary exercise policy
\bar{\mu}\equiv(\bar{\mu}_{1},\ldots,\bar{\mu}_{T})
which is feasible for the stochastic optimal stopping problem (
OPT
), and choose any arbitrary constant
\eta>0
. In our first claim, we show that this exercise policy can be approximated by an exercise policy with exercise regions that are open sets.

Claim 4

There exists an exercise policy
\tilde{\mu}^{\eta}\equiv(\tilde{\mu}_{1}^{\eta},\ldots,\tilde{\mu}_{T}^{\eta})
such that:

(a)

\mathbb{E}[g(\tau_{\tilde{\mu}^{\eta}}(x),x)]\geq\mathbb{E}[g(\tau_{\bar{\mu}}(x),x)]-\eta
;

(b)

For each period
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
,
A_{t}^{\eta}\triangleq\{y_{t}\in\mathcal{X}:\tilde{\mu}^{\eta}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\}
is an open set.

Proof C.8

Proof of Claim
4
. Because
\bar{\mu}_{1},\ldots,\bar{\mu}_{T}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\{\textsc{Stop},\textsc{Continue}\}
are feasible for the optimization problem (
OPT
), it follows from §
2.1
that
\bar{\mu}_{1},\ldots,\bar{\mu}_{T}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\{\textsc{Stop},\textsc{Continue}\}
are measurable functions. Thus, it follows for each period
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
that the set

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{A}_{t}}\triangleq\left\{y_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:{\mu}_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\right\}
is a Borel set in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Now, for each period
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
, let
\mathbb{P}_{x,t}(\cdot)\triangleq\mathbb{P}(x_{t}\in\cdot)
denote the marginal probability law of the stochastic process on period
t
. Since
{A}_{t}
is a Borel set, it is a well known result from measure theory
(
Rudin 1964
, Remark 11.11(b))
that there exists an open set
A_{t}^{\eta}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
which satisfies
A_{t}\subseteq A_{t}^{\eta}
and
\mathbb{P}_{x,t}(A_{t}^{\eta}\setminus A_{t})\leq\frac{\eta}{T}
. Using these sets, we define an exercise policy on each period
t
by

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}^{\eta}_{t}(y_{t})\triangleq\begin{cases}\textsc{Stop},&\text{if }y_{t}\in A^{\eta}_{t},\\
\textsc{Continue},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

It follows from the above construction that the new exercise policy
\tilde{\mu}^{\eta}\equiv(\tilde{\mu}^{\eta}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\mu}^{\eta}_{T})
satisfies property
(b)
of Claim
4
. To show that property
(a)
of Claim
4
holds, let us first define the following set for notational convenience:

\displaystyle B\triangleq\left\{y\equiv(y_{1},\ldots,y_{T}):y_{t}\notin A_{t}^{\eta}\setminus A_{t}\;\text{for all }t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}\right\}

We observe that
B
is a Borel set in
\mathbb{R}^{Td}
, since
B
is comprised of a finite number of complements and intersections of Borel sets. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\left|g(\tau_{\tilde{\mu}^{\eta}}(x),x)-g(\tau_{\bar{\mu}}(x),x)\right|]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left|g(\tau_{\tilde{\mu}^{\eta}}(x),x)-g(\tau_{\bar{\mu}}(x),x)\right|\mathbb{I}\left\{x\notin B\right\}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\left|g(\tau_{\tilde{\mu}^{\eta}}(x),x)-g(\tau_{\bar{\mu}}(x),x)\right|\mathbb{I}\left\{x\in B\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left|g(\tau_{\tilde{\mu}^{\eta}}(x),x)-g(\tau_{\bar{\mu}}(x),x)\right|\mathbb{I}\left\{x\notin B\right\}\right]+0

\displaystyle\leq U\mathbb{P}\left(x\notin B\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left(x_{t}\in A_{t}^{\eta}\setminus A_{t}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\eta.

The first equality follows from the law of total expectation. The second equality holds because
g(\tau_{\tilde{\mu}^{\eta}}(x),x)=g(\tau_{\bar{\mu}}(x),x)
when
x\in B
. The first inequality follows from Assumption
2.4
. The second inequality follows from the union bound and the definition of the set
B
. The third and final inequality holds because
\mathbb{P}(x_{t}\in A_{t}^{\eta}\setminus A_{t})=\mathbb{P}_{x,t}(A_{t}^{\eta}\setminus A_{t})\leq\frac{\eta}{T}
. This concludes our proof of Claim
4
.

\halmos

In the above Claim
4
, we showed that we can construct a new exercise policy
\tilde{\mu}^{\eta}\equiv(\tilde{\mu}^{\eta}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\mu}^{\eta}_{T})
which is close to the original exercise policy
\bar{\mu}\equiv(\bar{\mu}_{1},\ldots,\bar{\mu}_{T})
with respect to expected reward. However, the new exercise policy is comprised of exercise regions in each period which are open sets. We now use this open set property, along with Assumption
2.4
, to show the following second claim.

Claim 5

There exists an exercise policy
\breve{\mu}^{\eta}\equiv(\breve{\mu}_{1}^{\eta},\ldots,\breve{\mu}_{T}^{\eta})
such that:

(a)

\mathbb{E}[g(\tau_{\breve{\mu}^{\eta}}(x),x)]\geq\mathbb{E}[g(\tau_{\tilde{\mu}^{\eta}}(x),x)]-\eta
;

(b)

\lim_{y\to x}\tau_{\breve{\mu}^{\eta}}(y)=\tau_{\breve{\mu}^{\eta}}(x)
almost surely.

Proof C.9

Proof of Claim
5
.
For each period
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
, consider the exercise region
A_{t}^{\eta}\triangleq\{y_{t}\in\mathcal{X}:\tilde{\mu}^{\eta}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\}
corresponding to the exercise policy
\tilde{\mu}^{\eta}_{t}
. Since
A_{t}^{\eta}
is an open set, it is a well-known result from measure theory
(
Rudin 1964
, Remark 11.11(a))
that
A_{t}^{\eta}
is the union of a countable collection of open balls. That is, there exists a countable set of tuples
\{(y_{t}^{\eta,\ell},\epsilon_{t}^{\eta,\ell})\}_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\epsilon_{t}^{\eta,\ell}>0
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and the following equality is satisfied:

\displaystyle A_{t}^{\eta}=\lim_{k\to\infty}\underbrace{\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{k}\left\{y_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|y_{t}-y_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\eta,}\ell}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon_{t}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\eta,}\ell}\right\}}_{C^{\eta,k}_{t}}.

We observe from the above construction that
C^{\eta,k}_{t}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, is an increasing sequence of sets which converges to
A_{t}^{\eta}
. Consequently, we have
\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(x_{t}\in A_{t}^{\eta}\setminus C^{\eta,k}_{t}\right)=0
,
and so it follows from the definition of a limit that there exists a
k_{t}^{\eta}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(x_{t}\in A_{t}^{\eta}\setminus C^{\eta,k_{t}^{\eta}}_{t}\right)\leq\frac{\eta}{T}.

(27)

Using these sets, we define an exercise policy on each period
t
by

\displaystyle\breve{\mu}^{\eta}_{t}(y_{t})\triangleq\begin{cases}\textsc{Stop},&\text{if }y_{t}\in C^{\eta,k_{t}^{\eta}}_{t},\\
\textsc{Continue},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Since the inequality (
27
) holds for each period
t
, it follows from identical reasoning as the proof of property
(a)
of Claim
4
that the new exercise policy
\breve{\mu}^{\eta}\equiv(\breve{\mu}^{\eta}_{1},\ldots,\breve{\mu}^{\eta}_{T})
satisfies property
(a)
of Claim
5
.

Moreover, since the exercise regions
C^{\eta,k_{t}^{\eta}}_{t}
are unions of finite numbers of open balls, and since the Lebesgue measure of the boundaries of a finite number of open balls is equal to zero, it follows from Assumption
2.4
that the random state in each period
x_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
will be a strictly positive distance from the boundary of
C^{\eta,k_{t}^{\eta}}_{t}
with probability one. This concludes our proof of property
(b)
of Claim
5
, and thus concludes the proof of Claim
5
.

\halmos

We now combine Claims
4
and
5
to conclude our proof of Lemma
C.6
. Indeed, it follows from these claims that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[g(\tau_{\bar{\mu}}(x),x)\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[g(\tau_{\tilde{\mu}^{\eta}}(x),x)\right]+\eta\leq\mathbb{E}\left[g(\tau_{\breve{\mu}^{\eta}}(x),x)\right]+2\eta=\mathbb{E}\left[\liminf_{y\to x}g(\tau_{\breve{\mu}^{\eta}}(y),x)\right]+2\eta,

where the first inequality follows from property
(a)
of Claim
4
, the second inequality follows from property
(a)
of Claim
5
, and the third inequality follows from property
(b)
of Claim
5
. Since the exercise policy
\bar{\mu}\equiv(\bar{\mu}_{1},\ldots,\bar{\mu}_{T})
and constant
\eta>0
were chosen arbitrarily, we have proven that

\displaystyle\sup_{\mu}\mathbb{E}\left[g(\tau_{\bar{\mu}}(x),x)\right]\leq\sup_{\mu}\mathbb{E}\left[\liminf_{y\to x}g(\tau_{{\mu}}(y),x)\right].

The other direction of the inequality obviously holds, and so our proof of Lemma
C.6
is complete.

\halmos

We now combine the above novel intermediary lemmas to establish our proofs of Theorems
2.2
and
2.3
.

Proof C.10

Proof of Theorem
2.2
.
We first show that the optimal objective value of (
RO
) is an asymptotic lower bound on the optimal objective value of (
OPT
). Indeed,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\mu}\left\{\widehat{J}_{N,\epsilon}(\mu)-J^{*}(\mu)\right\}\quad\text{almost surely}

(28)

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left(\sup_{\mu}\widehat{J}_{N,\epsilon}(\mu)-\sup_{\mu}J^{*}(\mu)\right)

(29)

\displaystyle=-\sup_{\mu}J^{*}(\mu)+\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\mu}\widehat{J}_{N,\epsilon}(\mu)

(30)

\displaystyle=-\sup_{\mu}J^{*}(\mu)+\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\mu}\widehat{J}_{N,\epsilon}(\mu)\quad\text{almost surely},

(31)

where (
28
) follows from Theorem
2.4
, (
29
) and (
30
) follow from algebra, and (
31
) follows from Lemma
C.3
. Note that all of the above limits exist because
\epsilon\mapsto\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\mu}\{\widehat{J}_{N,\epsilon}(\mu)-J^{*}(\mu)\}
and
\epsilon\mapsto\limsup_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\mu}\widehat{J}_{N,\epsilon}(\mu)
are monotonic functions.

We next show that the optimal objective value of (
RO
) provides an asymptotic upper bound on the optimal objective value of (
OPT
). Indeed, we observe that

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\mu}\widehat{J}_{N,\epsilon}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\liminf_{N\to\infty}\sup_{\mu}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\inf_{y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}}g(\tau_{\mu}(y),x^{i})

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\sup_{\mu}\liminf_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\inf_{y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}}g(\tau_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mu}}}(y),x^{i})

(32)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\sup_{\mu}\mathbb{E}\left[\inf_{y\in\mathcal{X}^{T}:\;\|y-x\|\leq\epsilon}g(\tau_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mu}}}(y),x)\right]\quad\text{almost surely}

(33)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\sup_{\mu}\mathbb{E}\left[\liminf_{y\to x}g(\tau_{{\mu}}(y),x)\right]\quad\text{almost surely}}.

(34)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\sup_{\mu}\mathbb{E}\left[g(\tau_{\mu}(x),x)\right]}

(35)

(
32
) follows from algebra;
(
33
) follows from the strong law of large numbers; (
34
) follows from the dominated convergence theorem. We note that the strong law of large numbers and dominated convergence theorem can both be applied because of Assumption
2.4
. Finally, (
35
) follows from Lemma
C.6
. Combining the above, our proof of Theorem
2.2
is complete.
\halmos

Proof C.11

Proof of Theorem
2.3
.
We observe from Theorems
2.2
and
2.4
that for every arbitrary
\eta>0
, there exists a finite
\bar{\epsilon}(\eta)>0
almost surely such that the following statements hold for all
0<\epsilon<\bar{\epsilon}(\eta)
:

\displaystyle\left|\lim_{N\to\infty}\widehat{J}_{N,\epsilon}(\hat{\mu}_{N,\epsilon})-\sup_{\mu}J^{*}(\mu)\right|\leq\eta\quad\textnormal{almost surely};

(36)

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\left(J^{*}(\hat{\mu}_{N,\epsilon})-\widehat{J}_{N,\epsilon}(\hat{\mu}_{N,\epsilon})\right)\geq-\eta\quad\textnormal{almost surely}.

(37)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\sup_{\mu}J^{*}(\mu)\geq\limsup_{N\to\infty}J^{*}(\hat{\mu}_{N,\epsilon})\geq\liminf_{N\to\infty}J^{*}(\hat{\mu}_{N,\epsilon})\geq\liminf_{N\to\infty}\widehat{J}_{N,\epsilon}(\hat{\mu}_{N,\epsilon})-\eta\geq\sup_{\mu}J^{*}(\mu)-2\eta,

where the first inequality holds because each
\hat{\mu}_{N,\epsilon}
is a feasible but possibly suboptimal solution to (
OPT
), the second inequality is obvious, the third inequality follows from (
37
), and the final inequality follows from (
36
). Rearranging the above line, we have shown that the following statements hold for all
0<\epsilon<\bar{\epsilon}(\eta)
:

\displaystyle\left|\limsup_{N\to\infty}J^{*}(\hat{\mu}_{N,\epsilon})-\sup_{\mu}J^{*}(\mu)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\eta\quad\textnormal{almost surely};

\displaystyle\left|\liminf_{N\to\infty}J^{*}(\hat{\mu}_{N,\epsilon})-\sup_{\mu}J^{*}(\mu)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\eta\quad\textnormal{almost surely}.

Since
\eta>0
was chosen arbitrarily, our proof of Theorem
2.3
is complete.
\halmos

Appendix D
Proofs from §
3

Proof D.1

Proof of Lemma
3.2
.
We recall from §
2.1
that a Markovian stopping rule satisfies
\tau_{\mu}(y)=\infty
for a trajectory
y\equiv(y_{1},\ldots,y_{T})\in\mathcal{X}^{T}
if and only if
\mu_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Continue}
for each period
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
. Therefore, we observe the robust optimization problem (
ROT
) is equivalent to the following optimization problem:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\sup_{\mu}&&\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\inf_{y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}}g(\tau_{\mu}(y),x^{i})\\
&\textnormal{subject to}&&\tau_{\mu}(y)<\infty\quad\textnormal{for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\textnormal{ and }y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}\end{aligned}

(RO”)

Now consider any arbitrary exercise policy
\mu\equiv(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{T})
, and let
\mu^{\prime}\equiv(\mu^{\prime},\ldots,\mu^{\prime})
be an exercise policy that is defined for each period
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
and state
y_{t}\in\mathcal{X}
as

\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}_{t}(y_{t})\triangleq\begin{cases}\mu_{t}(y_{t}),&\text{if }t\in\{1,\ldots,T-1\},\\
\textsc{Stop},&\text{if }t=T.\end{cases}

We readily observe that
\tau_{\mu^{\prime}}(y)\leq T
for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and
y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}
, which implies that
\mu^{\prime}
satisfies the constraints of (
RO”
). Moreover, we observe for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and
y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}
that

\displaystyle\tau_{\mu^{\prime}}(y)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\tau_{\mu}(y),&\text{if }\tau_{\mu}(y)<\infty,\\
T,&\text{if }\tau_{\mu}(y)=\infty.\end{cases}

Since the reward function satisfies
g(1,y),\ldots,g(T,y)\geq 0
and
g(\infty,y)=0
for all
y\in\mathcal{X}^{T}
, we have shown for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and
y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}
that

\displaystyle g(\tau_{\mu}(y),x^{i})

\displaystyle=g(\tau_{\mu^{\prime}}(y),x^{i})

\displaystyle\textnormal{ if }\tau_{\mu}(y)<\infty,\textnormal{ and }

\displaystyle g(\tau_{\mu}(y),x^{i})

\displaystyle=0\leq g(T,x^{i})=g(\tau_{\mu^{\prime}}(y),x^{i})

\displaystyle\textnormal{ if }\tau_{\mu}(y)=\infty.

We thus conclude that the objective value
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\inf_{y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}}g(\tau_{\mu^{\prime}}(y),x^{i})
associated with the new exercise policy
\mu^{\prime}\equiv(\mu^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mu^{\prime}_{T})
is always greater than or equal to the objective value
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\inf_{y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}}g(\tau_{\mu}(y),x^{i})
associated with the original exercise policy
\mu\equiv(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{T})
. Since
\mu\equiv(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{T})
was chosen arbitrarily, our proof of Lemma
3.2
is complete.
\halmos

Proof D.2

Proof of Lemma
3.3
.
Consider any
\mu\equiv(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{T})
that satisfies the constraints of (
ROT
), and define

\displaystyle\sigma^{i}\triangleq\min\left\{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}:\;\mu_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\textnormal{ for all }y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\right\}\quad\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}.

It follows from the fact that
\mu
is feasible for (
ROT
) that
\sigma^{1},\ldots,\sigma^{N}\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
. Moreover, for each sample path
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and trajectory
y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}
, we observe that

\displaystyle\tau_{\mu}(y)

\displaystyle=\min\{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}:\mu_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\}\leq\sigma^{i},

where the equality is simply the definition of a Markovian stopping rule and the inequality follows from the fact that
\mu_{\sigma^{i}}(y_{\sigma^{i}})=\textsc{Stop}
for all
y_{\sigma^{i}}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{\sigma^{i}}
. Therefore, we observe for each sample path
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
that

\displaystyle\left\{\tau_{\mu}(y):y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}\}:\begin{aligned} &\textnormal{there exists }y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}\textnormal{ such that }\mu_{t^{\prime}}(y_{t^{\prime}})=\textsc{Continue}\\
&\textnormal{for all }t^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,t-1\}\textnormal{ and }\mu_{t}(y)=\textsc{Stop}\end{aligned}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}\}:\;\textnormal{there exists }y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\textnormal{ such that }\mu_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\right\}.

Indeed, the first equality follows from the definition of a Markovian stopping rule and from the fact that
\tau_{\mu}(y)\leq\sigma^{i}
for all
y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}
. The second equality follows from the fact that
\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}
is not a subset of
\{y_{t}\in\mathcal{X}:\mu_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\}
for each period
t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}-1\}
, which implies for each
t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}-1\}
that there exists a
y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}
that satisfies
\mu_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Continue}
. We thus conclude for each sample path
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
that

\displaystyle\inf_{y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}}g(\tau_{\mu}(y),x^{i})=\min\limits_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{i}):\textnormal{there exists }y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\textnormal{ such that }\mu_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\right\},

which completes our proof of Lemma
3.3
.
\halmos

Proof D.3

Proof of Lemma
3.5
.
Let
\mu^{\prime}
be a pruned version of
\mu
, and let
\sigma^{1},\ldots,\sigma^{N}
satisfy the following equalities for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
:

\displaystyle\sigma^{i}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}:\;\mu_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\;\forall y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\right\}=\min\left\{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}:\;\mu_{t}^{\prime}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\;\forall y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\right\}.

(38)

Then it follows from the fact that
\mu
is feasible for (
ROT
) that
\sigma^{1},\ldots,\sigma^{N}\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
. Therefore, for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
,

\displaystyle\inf_{y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}}g(\tau_{\mu^{\prime}}(y),x^{i})

\displaystyle=\min\limits_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{i}):\textnormal{there exists }y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\textnormal{ such that }\mu_{t}^{\prime}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\min\limits_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{i}):\textnormal{there exists }y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\textnormal{ such that }\mu_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\right\}

\displaystyle=\inf_{y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}}g(\tau_{\mu}(y),x^{i}).

Indeed, the two equalities follow from Lemma
3.3
and line (
38
). The inequality follows from the fact that
\mu^{\prime}
is a pruned version of
\mu
, which implies that
\left\{y_{t}\in\mathcal{X}:\mu_{t}^{\prime}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\right\}\subseteq\left\{y_{t}\in\mathcal{X}:\mu_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\right\}
for all
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
. Our proof of Lemma
3.5
is thus complete.

\halmos

Proof D.4

Proof of Lemma
3.6
.
Consider any
\mu\equiv(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{T})
that satisfies the constraints of (
ROT
), and define

\displaystyle\sigma^{i}\triangleq\min\left\{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}:\;\mu_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\textnormal{ for all }y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\right\}\quad\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}.

Our proof that
\mu^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}
is a pruned version of
\mu
is split into the following two intermediary claims.

Claim 6

\{y_{t}\in\mathcal{X}:\mu_{t}^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\}\subseteq\left\{y_{t}\in\mathcal{X}:\mu_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\right\}
for all
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
.

Proof D.5

Proof of Claim
6
.
Indeed, we observe for each period
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
that

\displaystyle\left\{y_{t}\in\mathcal{X}:\mu_{t}^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{y_{t}\in\mathcal{X}:y_{t}\in\bigcup\limits_{i:\;\sigma^{i}=t}\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\left\{y_{t}\in\mathcal{X}:y_{t}\in\bigcup\limits_{i:\;\sigma^{i}=t}\left\{y_{t}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}:\mu_{t}(y_{t}^{\prime})=\textsc{Stop}\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\left\{y_{t}\in\mathcal{X}:\mu_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\right\},&\text{if there exists }i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\textnormal{ such that }\sigma^{i}=t,\\
\emptyset,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\subseteq\left\{y_{t}\in\mathcal{X}:\mu_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\right\},

where the first equality follows from the definition of
\mu^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}_{t}
, the first inclusion follows from the fact that
\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\subseteq\{y_{t}\in\mathcal{X}:\mu_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\}
for each sample path
i
that satisfies
\sigma^{i}=t
, the second equality follows from algebra, and the second inclusion follows from algebra. This concludes our proof of Claim
6
.

\halmos

Claim 7

For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
,

\displaystyle\min\left\{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}:\;\mu_{t}^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\;\forall y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\right\}=\min\left\{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}:\;\mu_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\;\forall y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\right\}.

Proof D.6

Proof of Claim
7
.
Consider any sample path
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
. We first observe that

\displaystyle\min\left\{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}:\;\mu_{t}^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\;\forall y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sigma^{i}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}:\;\mu_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\;\forall y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\right\},

where the inequality follows from the fact that
\mu_{\sigma^{i}}^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}(y_{\sigma^{i}})=\textsc{Stop}
for all
y_{\sigma^{i}}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{\sigma^{i}}
, and the equality follows from the definition of
\sigma^{i}
. Moreover, it follows immediately from Claim
6
that

\displaystyle\min\left\{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}:\;\mu_{t}^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\;\forall y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\right\}\geq\min\left\{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}:\;\mu_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\;\forall y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\right\}.

Our proof of Claim
7
is thus complete.

\halmos

Combining Claims
6
and
7
with Definition
3.4
, we conclude that
\mu^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}
is a pruned version of
\mu
, which completes our proof of Lemma
3.6
.

\halmos

The following proof follows the identical reasoning as discussed in §
3.2
, and is stated formally here for the sake of completeness.

Proof D.7

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.
Lemma
3.2
shows that the robust optimization problem (
RO
) is equivalent to the robust optimization problem (
ROT
). Moreover, for any arbitrary exercise policy
\mu
that is feasible for (
ROT
), Lemmas
3.5
and
3.6
together show that there exists an exercise policy
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
such that the objective value associated with
\mu
is less than or equal to the objective value associated with
\mu^{\prime}
. Since
\mu
was chosen arbitrarily, our proof of Theorem
3.1
is complete.

\halmos

Proof D.8

Proof of Theorem
3.7
.
We split our proof into the following two intermediary claims.

Claim 8

The optimal objective value of (
RO
) is less than or equal to the optimal objective value of (
IP
).

Proof D.9

Proof of Claim
8
.
Consider any arbitrary
\mu\in\mathcal{M}
, and define the integers

\displaystyle\sigma^{i}\triangleq\min\left\{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}:\;\mu_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\textnormal{ for all }y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\right\}\quad\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}.

(39)

It follows from the definition of
\mathcal{M}
that
\mu
satisfies the constraints of (
ROT
), and so Lemma
3.6
implies that
\mu^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}
is a pruned version of
\mu
. Therefore, it follows from Definition
3.4
and line (
39
) that the following equalities hold:

\displaystyle\sigma^{i}=\min\left\{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}:\;\mu_{t}^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\textnormal{ for all }y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\right\}\quad\forall i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}.

(40)

Therefore, we observe that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\inf_{y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}}g\left(\tau_{\mu}(y),x^{i}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\inf_{y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}}g\left(\tau_{\mu^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}}(y),x^{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\min\limits_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{i}):\textnormal{there exists }y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\textnormal{ such that }\mu_{t}^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{i}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\sigma^{j}=t\right\},

(41)

where the first inequality follows from Lemma
3.5
, the first equality follows from Lemma
3.3
and line (
40
), and the second equality follows from the fact that
\mu^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}
if and only if there exists a sample path
j
such that
y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}
and
\sigma^{j}=t
. Because
\mu\in\mathcal{M}
was chosen arbitrarily, we conclude from line (
41
) and Theorem
3.1
that the optimal objective value of (
RO
) is less than or equal to the optimal objective value of (
IP
). Our proof of Claim
8
is thus complete.
\halmos

Claim 9

The optimal objective value of (
RO
) is greater than or equal to the optimal objective value of (
IP
). Furthermore, for any choice of integers
\sigma^{1},\ldots,\sigma^{N}\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
, the corresponding exercise policy
\mu^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}\equiv(\mu_{1}^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}},\ldots,\mu_{T}^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}})
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\inf_{y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}}g\left(\tau_{\mu^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}}(y),x^{i}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{i}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\sigma^{j}=t\right\}.

Proof D.10

Proof of Claim
9
.
Consider any arbitrary integers
\sigma^{1},\ldots,\sigma^{N}\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
. For each sample path
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, we observe that

\displaystyle\min\left\{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}:\;\mu_{t}^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\textnormal{ for all }y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}\}:\;\mu_{t}^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\textnormal{ for all }y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sigma^{i},

(42)

where the equality follows from the fact that
\mu^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}\equiv(\mu^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}_{1},\ldots,\mu^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}_{T})
by construction satisfies
\mu^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}_{\sigma^{i}}(y_{\sigma^{i}})=\textsc{Stop}
for all
y_{\sigma^{i}}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{\sigma^{i}}
, and the inequality follows from algebra. Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\inf_{y\in\mathcal{U}^{i}}g\left(\tau_{\mu^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}}(y),x^{i}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\min\limits_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{i}):\textnormal{there exists }y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\textnormal{ such that }\mu_{t}^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{i}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\sigma^{j}=t\right\},

(43)

where the inequality follows from Lemma
3.3
and line (
42
), and the equality follows from the fact that
\mu^{\sigma^{1}\cdots\sigma^{N}}_{t}(y_{t})=\textsc{Stop}
if and only if there exists a sample path
j
such that
y_{t}\in\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}
and
\sigma^{j}=t
. Because
\sigma^{1},\ldots,\sigma^{N}\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
were chosen arbitrarily, we conclude from line (
43
) and Theorem
3.1
that the optimal objective value of (
RO
) is greater than or equal to the optimal objective value of (
IP
), which concludes our proof of Claim
9
is complete.

\halmos

Combining Claims
8
and
9
, our proof of Theorem
3.7
is thus complete.

\halmos

Appendix E
Proof of Theorem
4.2

Our proof of the computational complexity of (
IP
) consists of a reduction from MIN-2-SAT, which is shown to be strongly NP-hard by
Kohli et al. 1994
:

MIN-2-SAT

The optimization version of MIN-2-SAT is to compute the optimal objective value of the binary linear optimization problem

\displaystyle v^{\textnormal{MIN-2-SAT}}\triangleq

\displaystyle\underset{b,z}{\textnormal{minimize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}z_{k}

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle z_{k}\geq b_{\ell}

\displaystyle\forall k\in\{1,\ldots,K\},\forall\ell\in I^{+}_{k}

\displaystyle z_{k}\geq 1-b_{\ell}

\displaystyle\forall k\in\{1,\ldots,K\},\forall\ell\in I^{-}_{k}

\displaystyle b_{\ell}\in\{0,1\}

\displaystyle\forall\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\},

where the given sets
I^{+}_{k},I^{-}_{k}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,L\}
satisfy
|I^{+}_{k}|+|I^{-}_{k}|=2
for each
k\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
.

Note that the following equality is obtained by replacing each decision variable
z_{k}
with
1-z_{k}
:

\displaystyle v^{\textnormal{MIN-2-SAT}}=\quad N\;\;-

\displaystyle\underset{b,z}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}z_{k}

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle z_{k}\leq 1-b_{\ell}

\displaystyle\forall k\in\{1,\ldots,K\},\forall\ell\in I^{+}_{k}

\displaystyle z_{k}\leq b_{\ell}

\displaystyle\forall k\in\{1,\ldots,K\},\forall\ell\in I^{-}_{k}

\displaystyle b_{\ell}\in\{0,1\}

\displaystyle\forall\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}.

We now show that any instance of the above maximization problem can be equivalently reformulated as polynomially-size instance of (
IP
) with
T=3
periods.

Proof E.1

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.
Consider any arbitrary instance of the binary linear optimization problem

\displaystyle\underset{b,z}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{K}z_{k}

(
\lnot
MIN-2-SAT)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle z_{k}\leq 1-b_{\ell}

\displaystyle\forall k\in\{1,\ldots,K\},\forall\ell\in I^{+}_{k}

\displaystyle z_{k}\leq b_{\ell}

\displaystyle\forall k\in\{1,\ldots,K\},\forall\ell\in I^{-}_{k}

\displaystyle b_{\ell}\in\{0,1\}

\displaystyle\forall\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\},

and let
e_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R}^{L+1}
denote the
\ell
-th column vector of the identity matrix. We construct an instance of (
IP
) defined as follows:

•

The number of periods is
T=3
.

•

The state space is
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{L+1}
.

•

The reward function for each period
t\in\{1,2,3\}
is
g(t,y)=y_{t}\cdot e_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L}+1}+K
.

•

The robustness parameter in the uncertainty sets is
\epsilon=\frac{2}{3}
.

•

The number of sample paths is
N\triangleq L+K
, and the sample paths are defined as follows:

–

For each
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
, let
x^{\ell}_{1}=x^{\ell}_{2}=e_{\ell}
and
x^{\ell}_{3}=-Ke_{L+1}
.

–

For each
k\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
, let
x^{L+k}_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell\in I^{+}_{k}}e_{\ell}
,
x^{L+k}_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell\in I^{-}_{k}}e_{\ell}
, and
x_{3}^{L+k}=e_{L+1}
.

In the remainder of the proof, we show that the above instance of (
IP
) is equivalent to (

¬

MIN-2-SAT
). Indeed, it follows immediately from the above construction that the values of
g(t,x^{i})
for each sample path
i
and period
t
are:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} g(1,x^{\ell})&=K;&g(2,x^{\ell})&=K;&g(3,x^{\ell})&=0,&&\forall\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\},\\
g(1,x^{L+k})&=K;&g(2,x^{L+k})&=K;&g(3,x^{L+k})&=K+1,&&\forall k\in\{1,\ldots,K\}.\end{aligned}

We require two intermediary claims:

Claim 10

There exists an optimal solution for (
IP
) which satisfies

\sigma^{1},\ldots,\sigma^{L}\in\{1,2\}\text{ and }\sigma^{L+1}=\cdots=\sigma^{L+K}=3.

Proof E.2

Proof of Claim
10
. We observe that the optimal objective value of (
IP
) is greater than or equal to
K
, since this objective value would be achieved by setting
\sigma^{1}=\cdots=\sigma^{N}=1
. Now consider any solution to (
IP
) where
\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}}=3
for some
\ell^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
. For that solution,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{i}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\sigma^{j}=t\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}:\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{\ell}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{\ell}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{\ell}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\sigma^{j}=t\right\}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{N}\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{\ell^{\prime}}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{\ell^{\prime}}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{\ell^{\prime}}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\sigma^{j}=t\right\}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{L+k}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{L+k}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{L+k}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\sigma^{j}=t\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}:\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}}K+0+\sum_{k=1}^{K}(K+1)

\displaystyle=K.

Because the objective value associated with this solution is never better than the objective value obtained by the solution
\sigma^{1}=\cdots=\sigma^{N}=1
, we have shown that there exists an optimal solution for (
IP
) that satisfies
\sigma^{1},\ldots,\sigma^{L}\in\{1,2\}
.

Consider any arbitrary solution
\sigma^{1},\ldots,\sigma^{N}\in\{1,2,3\}
that satisfies
\sigma^{1},\ldots,\sigma^{L}\in\{1,2\}
, and suppose that
\sigma^{L+k}\in\{1,2\}
for some
k\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
. To perform an exchange argument, we construct an alternative solution
\bar{\sigma}^{1},\ldots,\bar{\sigma}^{N}\in\{1,2,3\}
defined as

\bar{\sigma}^{i}\triangleq\begin{cases}\sigma^{i},&\text{if }i\neq L+k,\\
3,&\text{if }i=L+k.\end{cases}

We observe that the inclusion
\{j:\sigma^{j}\leq\sigma^{i}\}\supseteq\{j:\bar{\sigma}^{j}\leq\bar{\sigma}^{i}\}
holds for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\setminus\{L+k\}
. Moreover, we observe that

\displaystyle\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{L+k}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{L+k}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{L+k}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\sigma^{j}=t\right\}

\displaystyle=K,\;\text{and}

\displaystyle\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\bar{\sigma}^{L+k}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{L+k}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{L+k}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\bar{\sigma}^{j}=t\right\}

\displaystyle\in\{K,K+1\}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{i}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\sigma^{j}=t\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\bar{\sigma}^{i}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{i}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\bar{\sigma}^{j}=t\right\}

Because
\sigma^{1},\ldots,\sigma^{N}
was chosen arbitrarily, we conclude that there exists an optimal solution for (
IP
) which satisfies
\sigma^{1},\ldots,\sigma^{L}\in\{1,2\}
and
\sigma^{L+1}=\cdots=\sigma^{L+K}=3
. This concludes our proof of Claim
10
.

\halmos

Claim 11

If
\sigma^{L+1}=\cdots=\sigma^{L+K}=3
, then the following equality holds for each
k\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
:

\displaystyle\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{L+k}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{L+k}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{L+k}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\sigma^{j}=t\right\}

\displaystyle=K+\mathbb{I}\left\{\sigma^{\ell}=2\textnormal{ for all }\ell\in I^{+}_{k}\textnormal{ and }\sigma^{\ell}=1\textnormal{ for all }\ell\in I^{-}_{k}\right\}.

Proof E.3

Proof of Claim
11
.
For each
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
and
k\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
, we observe that

\displaystyle\left\|x^{\ell}_{1}-x^{L+k}_{1}\right\|_{\infty}=\left\|e_{\ell}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in I^{+}_{k}}e_{\ell^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2},&\text{if }\ell\in{I}^{+}_{k},\\
1,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

\displaystyle\left\|x^{\ell}_{2}-x^{L+k}_{2}\right\|_{\infty}=\left\|e_{\ell}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in I^{-}_{k}}e_{\ell^{\prime}}\right\|_{\infty}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2},&\text{if }\ell\in{I}^{-}_{k},\\
1,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

This implies that the set

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\ell}_{1}\cap\mathcal{U}^{L+k}_{1}=\left\{y_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{L+1}:\|y_{1}-x^{\ell}_{1}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{2}{3}\right\}\cap\left\{y_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{L+1}:\|y_{1}-x^{L+k}_{1}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{2}{3}\right\}

is nonempty if and only if
\ell\in{I}^{+}_{k}
, and the set

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\ell}_{2}\cap\mathcal{U}^{L+k}_{2}=\left\{y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{L+1}:\|y_{2}-x^{\ell}_{2}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{2}{3}\right\}\cap\left\{y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{L+1}:\|y_{2}-x^{L+k}_{2}\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{2}{3}\right\}

is nonempty if and only if
\ell\in{I}^{-}_{k}
. Consequently,
since
\sigma^{L+1}=\cdots=\sigma^{L+K}=3
, we conclude that the following equalities hold for all
k\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{L+k}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{L+k}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{L+k}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\sigma^{j}=t\right\}}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\min_{\ell\in{I}^{+}_{k}:\;\sigma^{\ell}=1}K,\min_{\ell\in{I}^{-}_{k}:\;\sigma^{\ell}=2}K,K+1\right\}

\displaystyle=K+\mathbb{I}\left\{\sigma^{\ell}=2\textnormal{ for all }\ell\in I^{+}_{k}\textnormal{ and }\sigma^{\ell}=1\textnormal{ for all }\ell\in I^{-}_{k}\right\}

This concludes the proof of Claim
11
.
\halmos

We now combine Claims
10
and
11
to complete our proof of Theorem
4.2
:

(
IP
)

(44)

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma^{1},\ldots,\sigma^{L}\in\{1,2\}\\
\sigma^{L+1}=\cdots=\sigma^{L+K}=3\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{L+K}\sum_{i=1}^{L+K}\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{i}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\sigma^{j}=t\right\}}

(45)

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma^{1},\ldots,\sigma^{L}\in\{1,2\}\\
\sigma^{L+1}=\cdots=\sigma^{L+K}=3\end{subarray}}\left\{\frac{1}{L+K}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}K\right.}

\displaystyle\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\left.\frac{1}{L+K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{L+k}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{L+k}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{L+k}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\sigma^{j}=t\right\}\right\}}

(46)

\displaystyle=\max_{\sigma^{1},\ldots,\sigma^{L}\in\{1,2\}}\left\{\frac{LK}{L+K}+\frac{1}{L+K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left(K+\mathbb{I}\left\{\sigma^{\ell}=2\text{ for all }\ell\in I^{+}_{k}\text{ and }\sigma^{\ell}=1\text{ for all }\ell\in I^{-}_{k}\right\}\right)\right\}

(47)

\displaystyle=K+\left(\frac{1}{L+K}\right)\max_{\sigma^{1},\ldots,\sigma^{L}\in\{1,2\}}\left\{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbb{I}\left\{\sigma^{\ell}=2\text{ for all }\ell\in I^{+}_{k}\text{ and }\sigma^{\ell}=1\text{ for all }\ell\in I^{-}_{k}\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=K+\left(\frac{1}{L+K}\right)*\eqref{prob:notmin2sat}.

(48)

Indeed, (
45
) follows from Claim
10
; (
46
) holds because
g(1,x^{\ell})=g(2,x^{\ell})=K
for all
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L\}
; (
47
) follows from Claim
11
; (
48
) follows from algebra and setting
b^{\ell}=0
if and only if
\sigma^{\ell}=2
. We have thus shown that any instance of MIN-2-SAT can be reduced to solving a polynomially-sized instance of (
IP
) with
T=3
, which concludes our proof of Theorem
4.2
.

\halmos

Appendix F
Reformulation of (
BP
) as Mixed-Integer Linear Optimization Problem

Zero-one bilinear programs can be transformed into equivalent mixed-integer linear optimization problems by introducing auxiliary decision variables
(
Adams and Sherali 1986
)
. In numerical experiments in §
5
, we perform such a linearization of the bilinear program (
BP
) by introducing auxiliary continuous decision variables
f^{i}_{t\ell}
which obey the constraints

\displaystyle f^{i}_{t\ell}\leq b^{i}_{t}

\displaystyle\textnormal{for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,N\},\;t\in\{1,\ldots,T\},\;\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L^{i}_{t}-1\}

\displaystyle f^{i}_{t\ell}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}-w^{i}_{t\ell}

\displaystyle\textnormal{for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,N\},\;t\in\{1,\ldots,T\},\;\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L^{i}_{t}-1\}

and replacing the objective function of (
BP
) with

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\underset{b,w,f}{\textnormal{maximize}}&&\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L^{i}_{t}-1}(\kappa^{i}_{\ell+1}-\kappa^{i}_{\ell})f^{i}_{t\ell}.\end{aligned}

To strengthen this linear relaxation of (
BP
), we also add the valid constraints:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &w^{i}_{1,1}=0&&\textnormal{for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\\
&b^{i}_{t}+w^{i}_{t,1}=w^{i}_{t+1,1}&&\textnormal{for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,N\},\;t\in\{1,\ldots,T-1\}\\
&b^{i}_{T}+w^{i}_{T,1}=1&&\textnormal{for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}.\end{aligned}

Indeed, the validity of the above constraints for (
BP
) follows from the fact that there is an optimal solution to this zero-one bilinear program which satisfies
\sum_{t=1}^{T}b^{i}_{t}=1
for each sample path
i
. In summary, this linearization procedure transforms (
BP
) into the following equivalent mixed-integer linear optimization problem:

\displaystyle\underset{b,w,f}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L^{i}_{t}-1}(\kappa^{i}_{\ell+1}-\kappa^{i}_{\ell})f^{i}_{t\ell}

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &f^{i}_{t\ell}\leq b^{i}_{t}&&\textnormal{for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,N\},\;t\in\{1,\ldots,T\},\;\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L^{i}_{t}-1\}\\
&f^{i}_{t\ell}\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}-w^{i}_{t\ell}&&\textnormal{for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,N\},\;t\in\{1,\ldots,T\},\;\ell\in\{1,\ldots,L^{i}_{t}-1\}\\
&w^{i}_{t,\ell}\leq w^{i}_{t+1,\ell}&&\textnormal{for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,N\},\;t\in\{1,\ldots,T-1\},\;\ell\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}^{i}|\}\\
&w^{i}_{t\ell}\leq w^{i}_{t,\ell+1}&&\textnormal{for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,N\},\;t\in\{1,\ldots,T\},\;\ell\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}^{i}|-1\}\\
&w^{i}_{1,1}=0&&\textnormal{for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\\
&b^{i}_{t}+w^{i}_{t,1}=w^{i}_{t+1,1}&&\textnormal{for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,N\},\;t\in\{1,\ldots,T-1\}\\
&b^{i}_{T}+w^{i}_{T,1}=1&&\textnormal{for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\\
&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}b^{i}_{t}\leq w^{i}_{t+1,1}}&&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textnormal{for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,N\},\;t\in\{1,\ldots,T-1\}}\\
&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}b^{j}_{t}\leq w^{i}_{t\ell}}&&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textnormal{for all }i,j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\textnormal{ and }t\in\{1,\dots,T\}\textnormal{ such that }g(t,x^{i})=\kappa^{i}_{\ell}}\\
&&&{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textnormal{and }\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset}\\
&b^{i}_{t}\in\{0,1\}&&\textnormal{for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,N\},\;t\in\{1,\dots,T\}\\
&w^{i}_{t\ell}\in\mathbb{R}&&\textnormal{for all }i\in\{1,\ldots,N\},\;t\in\{1,\ldots,T\},\;\ell\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}^{i}|\}.\end{aligned}

Appendix G
Proofs of Theorem
4.3
, Lemma
4.4
, and Lemma
4.5

Proof G.1

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.
Our proof of Lemma
4.4
is split into two intermediary steps.

In the first intermediary step of our proof of Lemma
4.4
, we show that every feasible solution for (
IP
) can be transformed into a feasible solution for the (
BP-1
) with the same objective value. Indeed,
consider any feasible solution
\sigma^{1},\ldots,\sigma^{N}\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
for the optimization problem (
IP
). From these integers, we can define binary variables
b^{i}_{t}\triangleq\mathbb{I}\left\{\sigma^{i}=t\right\}
for each sample path
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and period
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
. With this definition of a binary vector
b
, we observe for each sample path
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
that

\displaystyle\psi^{i}(b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}b^{i}_{t}\left(\prod_{s=1}^{t-1}(1-b^{i}_{s})\right)\min_{s\in\{1,\ldots,t\}}\left\{g(s,x^{i}):\textnormal{there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{i}_{s}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{s}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }b^{j}_{s}=1\right\}

(49)

\displaystyle=\min_{s\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}\}}\left\{g(s,x^{i}):\textnormal{there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{i}_{s}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{s}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }b^{j}_{s}=1\right\}

(50)

\displaystyle=\min\limits_{s\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}\}}\left\{g(s,x^{i}):\textnormal{there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{i}_{s}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{s}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\sigma^{j}=s\right\},

(51)

where line (
49
) follows from the definition of
\psi^{i}(b)
, and lines (
50
) and (
51
) follow from the fact that we have defined the binary variables to satisfy
b^{j}_{t}=\mathbb{I}\left\{\sigma^{j}=t\right\}
for each sample path
j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and period
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
. Because the above reasoning holds for each sample path
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, we have shown that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\psi^{i}\left(b\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{i}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\sigma^{j}=t\right\}.

(52)

Therefore, we conclude that every feasible solution for (
IP
) can be transformed into a feasible solution for (
BP-1
) with the same objective value.

In the second intermediary step of the proof of Lemma
4.4
, we show that if
b
is a feasible solution for (
BP-1
) and if
\sigma^{i}\triangleq\min\{\min\{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}:b^{i}_{t}=1\},T\}
for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{i}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\sigma^{j}=t\right\}\geq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\psi^{i}(b).

(53)

Indeed, consider any feasible solution
b
for (
BP-1
), and let us define a new binary vector
\bar{b}
as

\displaystyle\bar{b}^{i}_{t}\triangleq\begin{cases}b^{i}_{t},&\text{if }t\in\{1,\ldots,T-1\},\\
1,&\text{if }t=T.\end{cases}

It follows immediately from the above definition of
\bar{b}
and from the definition of the function
\psi^{i}(\cdot)
for each sample path
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
that

\displaystyle\psi^{i}\left(\bar{b}\right)-\psi^{i}\left({b}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\min\limits_{s\in\{1,\ldots,T\}}\left\{g(s,x^{i}):\textnormal{there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{i}_{s}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{s}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }b^{j}_{s}=1\right\}-0,&\text{if }b^{i}_{1}=\cdots=b^{i}_{T}=0,\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle\geq 0.

where the inequality holds because the reward function is nonnegative, that is,
g(t,x^{i})\geq 0
for each sample path
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and period
s\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
(see §
2.1
).
Therefore, we have proven that
\bar{b}
has the same or better objective value as
b
in the optimization problem (
BP-1
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\psi^{i}\left(b\right)\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\psi^{i}\left(\bar{b}\right).

(54)

Now, for each sample path
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, let us define the following integer:

\displaystyle\sigma^{i}\triangleq\min\left\{\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}}\left\{t:b^{i}_{t}=1\right\},T\right\}=\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}}\left\{t:\bar{b}^{i}_{t}=1\right\}.

Given the integers
\sigma^{1},\ldots,\sigma^{N}\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
defined above, it follows from identical reasoning as in lines (
49
), (
50
), (
51
), and (
52
) that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\psi^{i}\left(\bar{b}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\min_{t\in\{1,\ldots,\sigma^{i}\}}\left\{g(t,x^{i}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }\sigma^{j}=t\right\}.

Combining the above equality with (
54
), we have proven that line (
53
) holds, thereby completing our proof of the second intermediary step.

In conclusion, we have shown through the above two intermediary steps that every feasible solution for either (
IP
) or (
BP-1
) can be transformed into a feasible solution for the other problem with the same or better objective value, which implies that the optimal objective values of (
IP
) and (
BP-1
) are equal. Moreover, the inequality in Lemma
4.4
follows immediately from the second intermediary step. Thus, our proof of Lemma
4.4
is complete.

\halmos

Proof G.2

Proof of Lemma
4.5
.
Consider any sample path
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and binary vector
b
. For the sake of convenience, let us repeat below the linear optimization problem that is found in the statement of Lemma
4.5
for the given sample path
i
and binary vector
b
:

\displaystyle\underset{w^{i}}{\textnormal{maximize}}

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L^{i}_{t}-1}(\kappa^{i}_{\ell+1}-\kappa^{i}_{\ell})b^{i}_{t}(1-w^{i}_{t\ell})

(
3
)

\displaystyle\textnormal{subject to}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &w^{i}_{t\ell}\leq w^{i}_{t+1,\ell}&&\textnormal{for all }t\in\{1,\ldots,T-1\},\;\ell\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}^{i}|\}\\
&w^{i}_{t\ell}\leq w^{i}_{t,\ell+1}&&\textnormal{for all }t\in\{1,\ldots,T\},\;\ell\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}^{i}|-1\}\\
&b^{j}_{t}\leq w^{i}_{t\ell}&&\textnormal{for all }j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\textnormal{ and }t\in\{1,\dots,T\}\\
&&&\textnormal{such that }g(t,x^{i})=\kappa^{i}_{\ell}\textnormal{ and }\mathcal{U}^{i}_{t}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{t}\neq\emptyset\\
&b^{i}_{t}\leq w^{i}_{t+1,1}&&\textnormal{for all }\;t\in\{1,\ldots,T-1\}\\
&w^{i}_{t\ell}\in\mathbb{R}&&\textnormal{for all }\;t\in\{1,\ldots,T\},\;\ell\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}^{i}|\}.\end{aligned}

In the remainder of this proof, we show that the optimal objective value of (
3
) is equal to
\psi^{i}(b)
.

To this end, we begin by characterizing the decision variables
w^{i}
in (
3
) at optimality. Indeed, it follows from the construction of the constants
\kappa^{i}_{\ell}
that each quantity
\kappa^{i}_{\ell+1}-\kappa^{i}_{\ell}
is strictly positive. Therefore, since each
b^{j}_{t}
is binary, we readily observe that there always exists an optimal solution
w^{i}
for (
3
) where the following equality holds for each period
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
and each
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}^{i}|\}
:

\displaystyle w^{i}_{t\ell}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if }\left[b^{j}_{s}=0\textnormal{ for all }j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\textnormal{ and }s\in\{1,\dots,t\}\textnormal{ such that }g(s,x^{i})\leq\kappa^{i}_{\ell}\textnormal{ and }\mathcal{U}^{i}_{s}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{s}\neq\emptyset\right]\\
&\text{and }\left[b^{i}_{s}=0\textnormal{ for all }s\in\{1,\ldots,t-1\}\right],\\
1,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(55)

Now consider any optimal solution
w^{i}
for the optimization problem (
3
) for which the equality in (
55
) is satisfied for each period
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
and each
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}^{i}|\}
. For each period
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
, let
\tilde{\ell}^{i}_{t}
be defined as the smallest integer such that there exists a sample path
j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and a period
s\in\{1,\ldots,t\}
that satisfy
b^{j}_{s}=1
,
g(s,x^{i})=\kappa^{i}_{\tilde{\ell}^{i}_{t}}
, and
\mathcal{U}^{i}_{s}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{s}\neq\emptyset
.
12
12

12

If no such integer exists, then we assign
\tilde{\ell}^{i}_{t}
to be equal to
L^{i}_{t}
.

In particular, we observe from (
55
) that the quantity
\tilde{\ell}^{i}_{t}
is equal to the smallest integer such that
w^{i}_{t\tilde{\ell}^{i}_{t}}=1
. Hence, the optimal objective value of the optimization problem (
3
) is equal to:

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L^{i}_{t}-1}(\kappa^{i}_{\ell+1}-\kappa^{i}_{\ell})b^{i}_{t}(1-w^{i}_{t\ell})

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}:\;b^{i}_{t}=1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L^{i}_{t}-1}(\kappa^{i}_{\ell+1}-\kappa^{i}_{\ell})(1-w^{i}_{t\ell})

(56)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L^{i}_{t}-1}(\kappa^{i}_{\ell+1}-\kappa^{i}_{\ell})(1-w^{i}_{t\ell}),&\textnormal{if }b^{i}_{1}=\cdots=b^{i}_{t-1}=0\textnormal{ and }b^{i}_{t}=1\textnormal{ for }t\in\{1,\ldots,T\},\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(57)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\tilde{\ell}^{i}_{t}-1}(\kappa^{i}_{\ell+1}-\kappa^{i}_{\ell}),&\textnormal{if }b^{i}_{1}=\cdots=b^{i}_{t-1}=0\textnormal{ and }b^{i}_{t}=1\textnormal{ for }t\in\{1,\ldots,T\},\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(58)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\kappa^{i}_{\tilde{\ell}^{i}_{t}},&\textnormal{if }b^{i}_{1}=\cdots=b^{i}_{t-1}=0\textnormal{ and }b^{i}_{t}=1\textnormal{ for }t\in\{1,\ldots,T\},\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(59)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}b^{i}_{t}\left(\prod_{s=1}^{t-1}\left(1-b^{i}_{s}\right)\right)\kappa^{i}_{\tilde{\ell}^{i}_{t}}

(60)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}b^{i}_{t}\left(\prod_{s=1}^{t-1}\left(1-b^{i}_{s}\right)\right)\min_{s\in\{1,\ldots,t\}}\left\{g(s,x^{i}):\textnormal{ there exists }j\textnormal{ such that }\mathcal{U}^{i}_{s}\cap\mathcal{U}^{j}_{s}\neq\emptyset\textnormal{ and }b^{j}_{s}=1\right\}

(61)

\displaystyle=\psi^{i}(b).

Indeed, we observe that the optimal objective value of (
3
) is equal to
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L^{i}_{t}-1}(\kappa^{i}_{\ell+1}-\kappa^{i}_{\ell})b^{i}_{t}(1-w^{i}_{t\ell})
because
w^{i}
is an optimal solution for (
3
). Line (
56
) follows from algebra. Line (
57
) follows from (
55
), which implies that if
b^{i}_{t}=1
, then
w^{i}_{s\ell}=1
for all
s\in\{t+1,\ldots,T\}
and all
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,|\mathcal{K}^{i}|\}
. Line (
58
) follows from the definition of
\tilde{\ell}^{i}_{t}
and from (
55
). Line (
59
) follows from the fact that
\kappa^{i}_{1}=0
. Line (
60
) follows from algebra. Line (
61
) follows from the definition of
\tilde{\ell}^{i}_{t}
and the definition of the constants
\kappa^{i}_{1},\ldots,\kappa^{i}_{|\mathcal{K}^{i}|}
. The final equality follows from the definition of
\psi^{i}(b)
. This completes our proof of Lemma
4.5
.
\halmos

4.3

and

.
